\vartheta_{\underline{L},t}
.

8.
Application III. Number of points of affine quadratic hypersurfaces over
\mathbb{F}_{p}

In this section, we study the congruence (
1.11
), in particular, its solution number
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)
. In §
8.1
, we provide a formula for
p
being an arbitrary modulus, not necessarily a prime. The proof is based on Theorem
4.6
and finite Fourier analysis. Then in §
8.2
, we prove Theorem
1.5
, as a direct consequence of the above general formula.

8.1.
A formula for arbitrary modulus
c

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
G
be a nonsingular even integral symmetric matrix of size
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
m
be an integer, and
c
be a positive integer. If
\gcd(D,c)=1
, where
D=\det(G)
, then the solution number
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)
of the congruence

\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{T}}G\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathbf{x}\equiv m\bmod{c},\quad\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}/c\mathbb{Z}^{n}

is given by the formula

(8.1)

r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=c^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{c}_{K}\cdot\sum_{a=0}^{c-1}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{n(1-\widetilde{c}_{0})}{8}\right)(a,c)^{\frac{n}{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{\widetilde{a}}{\widetilde{c}}^{n}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{(a,c)}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{a}{c}\left(\frac{\widetilde{c}\cdot\widetilde{c}^{\prime}-1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{-1}\mathbf{v}-m\right)\right),

where
\widetilde{a}=a/(a,c)
,
\widetilde{c}=c/(a,c)
,
\widetilde{c}_{0}
is the odd part of
\widetilde{c}
, and
\widetilde{c}^{\prime}
is an integer satisfies
\widetilde{c}\cdot\widetilde{c}^{\prime}\equiv 1\bmod{\widetilde{a}N}
(
N
being the level of
G
).

Note that for fixed
G
and
c
, the abbreviations
\widetilde{a}
,
\widetilde{c}
,
\widetilde{c}_{0}
, and
\widetilde{c}^{\prime}
all depend on
a
, so we can not place them outside the sum over
a
.

Proof.

We define a map

\displaystyle f_{G,\mathbf{v}}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n}/c\mathbb{Z}^{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}+c\mathbb{Z}^{n}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{T}}G\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathbf{x}+c\mathbb{Z},

which is well-defined as can be checked directly. Moreover, we regard
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}
, bu abuse of notations, as a map from
\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. They are related by the following formula:

(8.2)

\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}/c\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{af_{G,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x})}{c}\right)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}}r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{am}{c}\right),

where
a
is an arbitrary integer. This can be seen by regrouping the terms of the left-hand side according to the value of
f_{G,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x})
. We carry over the notations in §
2.3
with
M=\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}
. Then
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z})
. For each
a+c\mathbb{Z}\in\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}
, let
\chi_{a}
be the character of
\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}
defined by
\chi_{a}(v)=\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{av}{c}\right)
(The
n=1
case of the characters introduced in the proof of Lemma
5.1
). It is immediate that the map from
\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}
to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}}
that sends
a+c\mathbb{Z}
to
\chi_{a}
is a group isomorphism. In these notations, (
8.2
) amounts to

\widehat{r_{G,\mathbf{v}}}(\chi_{-a})=\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}/c\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{af_{G,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x})}{c}\right).

Thus, expanding
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}
as a linear combination of
\chi_{-a}
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}
(the finite Fourier expansion), we find that

\displaystyle r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{r_{G,\mathbf{v}}}(\chi_{-a})\chi_{-a}(m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{G}_{G}(\widetilde{a}/\widetilde{c},\mathbf{c};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{v})\mathfrak{e}\left(-\frac{am}{c}\right),

where
\mathbf{c}=(c,c,\dots,c)
. Since
\gcd(D,c)=1
we have
\gcd(D,\widetilde{c})=1
for all
a
. Thus, inserting (
3.2
) and then the conclusion of Theorem
4.6
into the above formula, we obtain (
8.1
) as desired. (The term with
a=0
should be considered separately since Theorem
4.6
requires
a\neq 0
, or we can just choose another representative, e.g.,
a=c
.)
∎

As an immediate corollary, we have

\lvert r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)-c^{n-1}\rvert\leq c^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\sum_{d\mid c,\,d\neq c}d^{\frac{n}{2}}\phi(c/d),

where
\phi
is the Euler totient function.

Remark 8.2
.

The number
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)
, with
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}
and
c
being a prime power, plays an important role in the deduction of the well-known (classical) Siegel Theorem on solution numbers of integral quadratic forms; cf.
[
41
]
, or
[
27
, Chap. II, Section 9]
. Our formula can be used to derive explicit expressions of the local densities that appear in Siegel’s Theorem. For instance,
[
27
, Chap. II, Lemma 9.8]
can be seen as a special case of (
8.1
) after an elementary simplification. For local densities in more general context, see, for instance,
[
53
]
. Finally, it seems that our formula for
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)
, when
\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{0}
, can be used to derive explicit formulas for local densities of inhomogeneous quadratic forms (cf., e.g.,
[
39
]
), which we deem important for future work.

8.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.5

Since
p\nmid D
, we can set
c=p
in (
8.1
), and thus obtain

(8.3)

r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=p^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{p}_{K}\cdot\left(p^{\frac{n}{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{p}_{K}+\sum_{a=1}^{p-1}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{n(1-p_{0})}{8}\right)\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a}{p}_{K}^{n}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{a}{p}\left(\frac{pp^{\prime}-1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{-1}\mathbf{v}-m\right)\right)\right),

where
p_{0}=p
if
p
is odd and
p_{0}=1
if
p=2
, and where
p^{\prime}
(depending an both
p
and
a
) is an integer satisfying
pp^{\prime}\equiv 1\bmod{aN}
. We choose
p^{\prime}
such that
pp^{\prime}\equiv 1\bmod{(p-1)!\cdot N}
. Then
pp^{\prime}\equiv 1\bmod{aN}
for all
1\leq a\leq p-1
, namely, this choice of
p^{\prime}
is the same for all
a
. Note that
\frac{pp^{\prime}-1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{-1}\mathbf{v}-m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case (1)

2\mid n
and
p>2
. The sum
\sum_{a=1}^{p-1}
in (
8.3
) is then a geometric sum. Therefore, if
\frac{pp^{\prime}-1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{-1}\mathbf{v}\equiv m\bmod{p}
, then
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=p^{n-1}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{p}_{K}\cdot\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{n(1-p)}{8}\right)\cdot p^{\frac{n}{2}-1}(p-1)
. On the other hand, if
\frac{pp^{\prime}-1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{-1}\mathbf{v}\not\equiv m\bmod{p}
, then
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=p^{n-1}-\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{p}_{K}\cdot\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{n(1-p)}{8}\right)\cdot p^{\frac{n}{2}-1}
. It is immediate that

\frac{pp^{\prime}-1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{-1}\mathbf{v}\equiv m\bmod{p}\Longleftrightarrow Nm+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{\bot}\mathbf{v}\equiv 0\bmod{p},

from which Theorem
1.5
(1) follows.

Case (2)

2\mid n
and
p=2
. Then (
8.3
) becomes

r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=2^{n-1}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{2}_{K}\cdot(-1)^{\frac{pp^{\prime}-1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{-1}\mathbf{v}-m}\cdot 2^{\frac{n}{2}-1}.

Since
p=2
, we have
2\nmid N
, and hence

(-1)^{\frac{pp^{\prime}-1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{-1}\mathbf{v}-m}=(-1)^{Nm+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{\bot}\mathbf{v}}

as in the previous case. Therefore, Theorem
1.5
(2) holds.

Case (3)

2\nmid n
. Then
2\mid N
(see
[
17
, Remarks 14.3.23]
), and hence
p>2
. Now (
8.3
) becomes

r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=p^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{p}_{K}\cdot\left(p^{\frac{n}{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{p}_{K}+\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{n(1-p)}{8}\right)\sum_{a=1}^{p-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a}{p}_{K}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{at_{m}^{\prime}}{p}\right)\right),

where
t_{m}^{\prime}=\frac{pp^{\prime}-1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{T}}G^{-1}\mathbf{v}-m
. As in the previous cases, we have
t_{m}^{\prime}\equiv-t_{m}\bmod{p}
. By the classical formula on Gauss character sums (cf., e.g.,
[
17
, Prop. 3.4.5(a) and 3.4.10(b)]
), we have

r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=p^{n-1}+p^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D}{p}_{K}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{n(1-p)}{8}\right)\sqrt{p}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-2t_m}{p}_{K}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{1-p}{8}\right),

from which Theorem
1.5
(3) follows.

9.
Application IV. Number of solutions of generalized Markoff equations over
\mathbb{F}_{p}

Here we carry over the notations introduced in §
1.5
and Theorem
1.6
. The aim of this section is to prove Theorem
1.6
. Besides
A_{11}
,
A_{22}
and
A_{33}
, we further set

(9.1)

A_{12}=-2a_{12}a_{33}+a_{13}a_{23},\quad A_{13}=a_{12}a_{23}-2a_{22}a_{13},\quad A_{23}=-2a_{11}a_{23}+a_{12}a_{13},

so that
G\cdot A=A\cdot G=D\cdot I_{3}
, where
A=(A_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}
with
A_{ij}=A_{ji}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

We have

(9.2)

\displaystyle A_{22}A_{33}-A_{23}^{2}

\displaystyle=D\cdot 2a_{11},\qquad

\displaystyle-A_{12}A_{33}+A_{23}A_{13}

\displaystyle=D\cdot a_{12},

(9.3)

\displaystyle A_{11}A_{33}-A_{13}^{2}

\displaystyle=D\cdot 2a_{22},\qquad

\displaystyle A_{12}A_{23}-A_{22}A_{13}

\displaystyle=D\cdot a_{13},

(9.4)

\displaystyle A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}

\displaystyle=D\cdot 2a_{33},\qquad

\displaystyle-A_{11}A_{23}+A_{12}A_{13}

\displaystyle=D\cdot a_{23},

and

(9.5)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=2a_{11}A_{11}+a_{12}A_{12}+a_{13}A_{13},

(9.6)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=a_{12}A_{12}+2a_{22}A_{22}+a_{23}A_{23},

(9.7)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=a_{13}A_{13}+a_{23}A_{23}+2a_{33}A_{33}.

Proof.

A straightforward calculation.
∎

The proof for the case
p=2
is relatively easy:

Lemma 9.2
.

Theorem
1.6
holds in the case
p=2
.

Proof.

Since we have assumed
p\nmid a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}d
, we can assume
a_{11}=a_{22}=a_{33}=d=1
for
p=2
without loss of generality. There are 8 possibilities of the value of
(a_{12}\bmod{2},a_{13}\bmod{2},a_{23}\bmod{2})
. For each value, we solve (
1.12
) modulo
2
by checking whether each value of
(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}/2\mathbb{Z}^{3}
satisfies it directly. In this way we find that Theorem
1.6
(4), i.e., the case
p=2
of Theorem
1.6
, holds.
∎

To prove Theorem
1.6
for
p>2
, we partition the affine variety determined by (
1.12
) into the sections
z=j
, where
j=0,1,\dots,p-1
, and then show at most two of these sections are singular affine quadratic varieties, and the rest are nonsingular ones.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
p
be an odd prime with
p\nmid a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}d
, and let
t_{1},t_{2}
be nonnegative integers. Set
a_{11}^{\prime}=a_{11}+pt_{1}
,
a_{22}^{\prime}=a_{22}+pt_{2}
. For
z=0,1,\dots,p-1
, set

\displaystyle G_{z}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{matrix}{2a_{11}^{\prime}}&{a_{12}-dz}\\
{a_{12}-dz}&{2a_{22}^{\prime}}\end{matrix}\right),\quad

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{z}

\displaystyle=(a_{13}z,a_{23}z),

\displaystyle D_{z}

\displaystyle=\det(G_{z})=4a_{11}^{\prime}a_{22}^{\prime}-(a_{12}-dz)^{2},\quad

\displaystyle N_{z}

\displaystyle=\text{ level of }G_{z}.

Then there exist
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
such that:

(1)

G_{z}
is positive definite for all
0\leq z\leq p-1
,

(2)

N_{z}=D_{z}
for all
0\leq z\leq p-1
,

(3)

The solution number of (
1.12
) modulo
p
is equal to

(9.8)

\sum_{z=0}^{p-1}r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2}),

where
r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2})
is defined in Theorem
1.5
.

Proof.

If
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are sufficiently large, then
G_{z}
is positive definite for all
0\leq z\leq p-1
. Among these large values we choose
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
such that
\gcd(a_{11}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime})=1
, which is possible by Dirichlet’s theorem on primes in arithmetic progressions, or by more elementary
[
17
, Lemma 6.1.1]
. We have

G_{z}^{-1}=D_{z}^{-1}\left(\begin{matrix}{2a_{22}^{\prime}}&{-(a_{12}-dz)}\\
{-(a_{12}-dz)}&{2a_{11}^{\prime}}\end{matrix}\right).

Therefore
D_{z}G_{z}^{-1}
is even integral, and hence
N_{z}\mid D_{z}
. Since
a_{11}^{\prime}
and
a_{22}^{\prime}
are coprime,
mG_{z}^{-1}
is not even integral for any proper positive divisor
m
of
D_{z}
, so
N_{z}=D_{z}
.

Note that the solution number of (
1.12
) modulo
p
is equal to the solution number of it with
a_{11}
,
a_{22}
replaced by
a_{11}^{\prime}
,
a_{22}^{\prime}
, respectively, since
a_{jj}\equiv a_{jj}^{\prime}\bmod{p}
. The latter equation can be rewritten as

\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}x&y\end{pmatrix}G_{z}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}+\mathbf{v}_{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}\equiv-a_{33}z^{2}\bmod{p},

from which (
9.8
) follows. (This formula holds for whatever
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.)
∎

From here on,
a_{11}^{\prime}
and
a_{22}^{\prime}
are always chosen, and
G_{z}
,
\mathbf{v}_{z}
,
D_{z}
, and
N_{z}
are always defined as in Lemma
9.3
. The sections
z=j
considered above are divided into three disjoint classes:

(9.9)

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{z\in\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}\colon N_{z}\equiv 0\bmod{p}\right\},

(9.10)

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{z\in\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}\colon N_{z}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p},\quad-N_{z}a_{33}z^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}_{z}^{\mathrm{T}}N_{z}G_{z}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{z}\equiv 0\bmod{p}\right\},

(9.11)

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle=\left\{z\in\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}\colon N_{z}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p},\quad-N_{z}a_{33}z^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}_{z}^{\mathrm{T}}N_{z}G_{z}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{z}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}\right\}.

For
z\in S_{2}
or
z\in S_{3}
,
r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2})
can be evaluated
11
11

11

This will be the only place where we apply our main theorem. After applying it, we will obtain some intermediate formulas for the solution numbers as one will see in the proof below (e.g. (
9.15
)). However, to acquire a formula in a relatively beautiful form like the one given in Theorem
1.6
, we need more effort. This extra effort is a lengthy, tedious, and elementary reasoning, which is split into fifteen cases.
using Theorem
1.5
(1), where
\delta
equals
1
or
0
, respectively. For
z\in S_{1}
, the following elementary fact will be used:

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
p
be an odd prime, let
G=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{2g_{11}}&{g_{12}}\\
{g_{12}}&{2g_{22}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, where
g_{11},g_{12},g_{22}\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Suppose that
p\mid\det(G)
, and
p\nmid g_{11}
. Then

(1)

If
p\nmid g_{12}v_{1}-2g_{11}v_{2}
, then
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=p
.

(2)

If
p\mid g_{12}v_{1}-2g_{11}v_{2}
, then

r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=p\cdot\left(1+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{4g_{11}m+v_1^2}{p}_{K}\right).

Proof.

For
G
,
\mathbf{v}
given above, the congruence (
1.11
) becomes

\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}x&y\end{pmatrix}\left(\begin{matrix}{2g_{11}}&{g_{12}}\\
{g_{12}}&{2g_{22}}\end{matrix}\right)\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&v_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}\equiv m\bmod{p}.

Multiplying
4g_{11}
on both sides, and noting that
4g_{11}g_{22}y^{2}\equiv g_{12}^{2}y^{2}\bmod{p}
(since
p\mid\det(G)
), we find this congruence is equivalent to

(9.12)

(2g_{11}x+g_{12}y+v_{1})^{2}\equiv 2(g_{12}v_{1}-2g_{11}v_{2})y+4g_{11}m+v_{1}^{2}\bmod{p}.

If
p\nmid g_{12}v_{1}-2g_{11}v_{2}
, then there are exactly
(p-1)/2
values for
y
in
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
such that the right-hand side of (
9.12
) is a quadratic residue mod
p
, and if
y
assumes each such value, there are exactly two choices of
x
such that (
9.12
) holds. On the other hand, there are exact one value for
y
such that the right-hand side of (
9.12
) is congruent to
0\bmod{p}
, and if
y
assumes this value, there is exactly one choice of
x
such that (
9.12
) holds. Therefore,

r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)=\frac{p-1}{2}\cdot 2+1\cdot 1=p,

which proves the first assertion.

If
p\mid g_{12}v_{1}-2g_{11}v_{2}
, then (
9.12
) is equivalent to

(2g_{11}x+g_{12}y+v_{1})^{2}\equiv 4g_{11}m+v_{1}^{2}\bmod{p}.

The rest of the proof is then immediate.
∎

The congruence appearing in (
9.10
) is connected to
A_{11}
and
A_{22}
as the following lemma shows:

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
p
be an odd prime with
p\nmid a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}d
. Then
-N_{z}a_{33}z^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}_{z}^{\mathrm{T}}N_{z}G_{z}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{z}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
holds if and only if
z\equiv 0\bmod{p}
or

(9.13)

(a_{13}a_{23}-2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz))^{2}\equiv A_{11}A_{22}\bmod{p}.

Proof.

First using Lemma
9.3
(2) to replace
N_{z}
by
D_{z}
, and then a straightforward calculation.
∎

The following classical formula will be used when we evaluate the sum (
9.8
):

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
p
be an odd prime, and let
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
with
p\nmid a
. Then

\sum_{x=0}^{p-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{ax^2+bx+c}{p}_{K}=\begin{cases}-\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a}{p}_{K}&\text{ if }b^{2}-4ac\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}\\
(p-1)\cdot\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a}{p}_{K}&\text{ if }b^{2}-4ac\equiv 0\bmod{p}.\end{cases}

Proof.

See
[
6
, Theorem 2.1.2]
and the example following it.
∎

The proof of Theorem
1.6
(for
p>2
) will be split into the following cases (all congruences are modulo
p
)

Case (1)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=-1.

Case (2)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=0,\quad A_{12}\equiv 0.

Case (3)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=0,\quad A_{12}\not\equiv 0.

Case (4)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\not\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\equiv 0.

Case (5)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\not\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0.

Case (6)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\not\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\not\equiv 0.

Case (7)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\not\equiv 0.

Case (8)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\not\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\equiv 0.

Case (9)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\equiv 0,

\displaystyle A_{33}\equiv 0.

Case (10)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\equiv 0,

\displaystyle A_{33}\not\equiv 0.

Case (11)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\not\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\not\equiv 0.

Case (12)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\not\equiv 0.

Case (13)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\not\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\equiv 0.

Case (14)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\equiv 0,

\displaystyle A_{33}\equiv 0.

Case (15)

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1,\quad a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22},\quad A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0,\quad a_{13}\equiv 0,\quad A_{23}\equiv 0,

\displaystyle A_{33}\not\equiv 0.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
p
be an odd prime with
p\nmid a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}d
. The above fifteen cases are mutually exclusive, and cover all possibilities. Moreover, Cases (7), (10), (12), (13), (14) cannot occur. Finally,
p\mid D
if and only if one of the cases (2), (4), (6), (8), (9) occurs, and
p\nmid D
if and only if one of the cases (1), (3), (5), (11), (15) occurs.

Proof.

It is immediate that these cases are mutually exclusive, and cover all possibilities.

To prove Case (7) is impossible, we assume its conditions hold, and we shall deduce a contradiction. Subtracting (
9.6
) from (
9.5
), and noting
a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22}
, we find that
a_{13}A_{13}\equiv a_{23}A_{23}
. Since
a_{13}\equiv 0
and
A_{23}\not\equiv 0
, we then have
a_{23}\equiv 0
. Thus, by the third equation in (
9.1
), we have
A_{23}\equiv 0
, a contradiction, which shows Case (7) is impossible.

For Case (10), assume by contradiction that it holds. Then
D\equiv 0
by
A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\equiv 0
and the first formula of (
9.4
). By the first formula of (
9.2
) and
A_{23}\equiv 0
we know that
A_{22}A_{33}\equiv 0
, which contradicts the assumptions
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
and
A_{33}\not\equiv 0
. This shows Case (10) never occurs.

For Case (12), assume by contradiction that it holds. Since
a_{13}\equiv 0
, we have
A_{22}\equiv 4a_{11}a_{33}
by the definition of
A_{22}
. Thus
a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22}\equiv 4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}
, which implies
A_{11}\equiv 4a_{22}a_{33}
. By this and the definition of
A_{11}
, we have
a_{23}\equiv 0
. It follows from this, the assumption
a_{13}\equiv 0
, and the third equation of (
9.1
) that
A_{23}\equiv 0
, which contradicts one of the assumptions. This shows Case (12) never occurs.

For Case (13), assume by contradiction that it holds. As in Case (7), we have
a_{13}A_{13}\equiv a_{23}A_{23}
. This, together with the assumptions
a_{13}\not\equiv 0
and
A_{23}\equiv 0
, implies
A_{13}\equiv 0
. By the second equation of (
9.3
), we then have
Da_{13}\equiv 0
. However,
D\not\equiv 0
(since
A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0
and the first equation of (
9.4
)), so
a_{13}\equiv 0
, which contradicts one of the assumptions. This shows Case (13) never occurs.

For Case (14), assume by contradiction that it holds. Since
A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0
we have
D\not\equiv 0
by the first equation of (
9.4
). By the assumptions
a_{13}\equiv 0
,
A_{23}\equiv 0
, and
A_{33}\equiv 0
, we have
D=a_{13}A_{13}+a_{23}A_{23}+2a_{33}A_{33}\equiv 0
(according to (
9.7
)), contradicts
D\not\equiv 0
just deduced. This shows Case (14) never occurs.

We now examine other cases to see whether
p\mid D
or not. In Case (1), it follows from the first equation of (
9.4
) that
p\nmid D
. In Cases (2) and (3), it follows again from the first equation of (
9.4
) that
p\mid D
and
p\nmid D
, respectively. In Cases (4), (5), (6), (8), (9), (11), (15), there are assumptions on
A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\bmod{p}
. By the first equation of (
9.4
), we have
p\mid D
if and only if
p\mid A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}
. Thus, in these cases, whether
p\mid D
or not is clear.
∎

In Cases (1)–(5), the following observation will significantly simplify the proof of Theorem
1.6
by reducing the number of subcases.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
p
be an odd prime with
p\nmid a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}d
, and assume we are in one of the cases (1)–(5). Then each element
z\in S_{1}
(see (
9.9
)), if exists, satisfies

p\nmid-da_{13}z^{2}+A_{23}z\quad\text{ or }\quad p\mid A_{22}z^{2},

and (as a consequence) we have
r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2})=p
.

Proof.

Assume that
p\mid-da_{13}z^{2}+A_{23}z
, we need to prove
p\mid A_{22}z^{2}
. Since
z\in S_{1}
, we have
(a_{12}-dz)^{2}\equiv 4a_{11}a_{22}\bmod{p}
(see Lemma
9.3
(2)).

If
z=0
, then obviously
p\mid A_{22}z^{2}
.

If
z\neq 0
, it remains to prove
p\mid A_{22}
. In this case
da_{13}z\equiv A_{23}\bmod{p}
. Hence we have

4a_{11}a_{22}a_{13}^{2}\equiv(a_{12}-dz)^{2}a_{13}^{2}\equiv(a_{12}a_{13}-A_{23})^{2}\bmod{p}.

Inserting the third equation of (
9.1
) into above, we obtain
4a_{11}a_{22}a_{13}^{2}\equiv 4a_{11}^{2}a_{23}^{2}\bmod{p}
, which is equivalent to
A_{11}a_{13}^{2}\equiv A_{22}a_{23}^{2}\bmod{p}
by the definitions of
A_{11}
and
A_{22}
. Assume by contradiction that
p\nmid A_{22}
, then
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{13}^2}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{23}^2}{p}_{K}
. If we are in Case (1), then
-\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{13}^2}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{23}^2}{p}_{K}
, so
a_{13}\equiv a_{23}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. If follows from this and the definitions of
A_{11}
and
A_{22}
that

\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{4a_{22}a_{33}\cdot 4a_{11}a_{33}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{11}a_{22}}{p}_{K}=1\text{ or }0,

which contradicts the assumption of Case (1). If we are in Cases (2) or (3), then
p\mid A_{11}
, and hence
p\mid a_{23}
by
A_{11}a_{13}^{2}\equiv A_{22}a_{23}^{2}\bmod{p}
. Since
A_{11}=4a_{22}a_{33}-a_{23}^{2}
, we deduce
p\mid 4a_{22}a_{33}
, a contradiction. Finally, if we are in Cases (4) or (5), then

\displaystyle A_{11}a_{13}^{2}\equiv A_{22}a_{23}^{2}\bmod{p}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow A_{11}(4a_{11}a_{33}-A_{22})\equiv A_{22}(4a_{22}a_{33}-A_{11})\bmod{p}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22}\bmod{p},

which contradicts one of the assumptions of Cases (4) and (5). We have thus shown that in each of the cases (1)–(5), there is a contradiction. Therefore we must have
p\mid A_{22}
.

We now prove the last assertion. Setting
G=G_{z}
,
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{z}
in Lemma
9.4
, we find that
g_{12}v_{1}-2g_{11}v_{2}\equiv-da_{13}z^{2}+A_{23}z\bmod{p}
. If
-da_{13}z^{2}+A_{23}z\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, then
r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2})=p
by Lemma
9.4
(1). On the other hand, if
-da_{13}z^{2}+A_{23}z\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, then
p\mid A_{22}z^{2}
by what we have just proved. By Lemma
9.4
(2), we have

r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2})=p\cdot\left(1+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{4g_{11}m+v_1^2}{p}_{K}\right)=p\cdot\left(1+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}z^2}{p}_{K}\right)=p.

This concludes the whole proof.
∎

We are now in a position to achieve our goal:

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

The case
p=2
has been proved in Lemma
9.2
. For
p>2
, let
R
denote the number of solutions of (
1.12
) over
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
. By Lemma
9.3
(3) we have

R=\sum_{z\in S_{1}}r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2})+\sum_{z\in S_{2}}r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2})+\sum_{z\in S_{3}}r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2}),

where
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
, and
S_{3}
are the sets defined in (
9.9
), (
9.10
), and (
9.11
), respectively. Set
r_{z}=r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2})
for simplicity. Inserting the formula given in Theorem
1.5
(1) into
r_{G_{z},\mathbf{v}_{z}}(-a_{33}z^{2})
for
z\in S_{2}\cup S_{3}
, we obtain

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in S_{1}}r_{z}+\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\left(p+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D_z}{p}_{K}(-1)^{\frac{1-p}{2}}(p-1)\right)+\sum_{z\in S_{3}}\left(p-\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D_z}{p}_{K}(-1)^{\frac{1-p}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=p^{2}-(-1)^{\frac{1-p}{2}}\sum_{z=0}^{p-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D_z}{p}_{K}+(-1)^{\frac{1-p}{2}}p\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D_z}{p}_{K}+\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p+(-1)^{\frac{1-p}{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D_z}{p}_{K}\right)

(9.14)

\displaystyle=p^{2}-(-1)^{\frac{1-p}{2}}\sum_{z=0}^{p-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D_z}{p}_{K}+p\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}+\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right),

where the last equality follows from the fact
D_{z}=N_{z}
(see Lemma
9.3
(2)).
Note that

\sum_{z=0}^{p-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{D_z}{p}_{K}=\sum_{z=0}^{p-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{4a_{11}a_{22}-(a_{12}-dz)^2}{p}_{K}=\sum_{z=0}^{p-1}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{4a_{11}a_{22}-z^2}{p}_{K}=-\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-1}{p}_{K},

where the last equality follows from Lemma
9.6
. Inserting this into (
9.14
) gives

(9.15)

R=p^{2}+1+p\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}+\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right).

The rest of the proof is split into Cases (1)–(15) introduced above, in each of which we shall evaluate the two sums in the right-hand side of (
9.15
) in terms of
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{jj}}{p}_{K}\cdot p
,
j=1,2,3
.

Note that in Cases (1)–(5), (
9.15
) can be further simplified to

(9.16)

R=p^{2}+1+p\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}

by Lemma
9.8
.

Case (1).
We have
p\nmid D
by Lemma
9.7
. Comparing (
9.16
) and Theorem
1.6
(1), we need to prove

(9.17)

\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}.

Since
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=-1
, (
9.13
) is unsolvable, and hence

(9.18)

S_{2}=\left\{z\in\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}\colon 4a_{11}a_{22}-(a_{12}-dz)^{2}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}\right\}\cap\{0\}.

If
A_{33}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, then
S_{2}=\{0\}
, and hence
\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_0}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}
, from which (
9.17
) follows. If
A_{33}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, then
S_{2}=\emptyset
, in which case (
9.17
) also holds.

Case (2).
We have
p\mid D
by Lemma
9.7
. The two assumptions of this case imply (
9.13
) has exactly one solution
z\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, and hence
S_{2}
is again given by (
9.18
). If
A_{33}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, then at least two of
A_{11},A_{22},A_{33}
are divisible by
p
, so we are in the case of Theorem
1.6
(2), whose conclusion holds by (
9.16
) since here
S_{2}=\emptyset
. On the other hand, suppose
A_{33}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Then
S_{2}=\{0\}
and hence
R=p^{2}+1+p\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}
. The situation
A_{11}\equiv A_{22}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
cannot happen, for if it happens, then
A_{13}\equiv A_{23}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
by the first equations of (
9.2
) and (
9.3
), and hence
A_{33}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
by (
9.7
), which contradicts the assumption
A_{33}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Therefore, at most one of
A_{11},A_{22},A_{33}
is divisible by
p
, that is, we are in the case of Theorem
1.6
(3). Without loss of generality, we assume that
A_{11}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, so
A_{22}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. By (
9.2
), we find that
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}
, and hence the conclusion of Theorem
1.6
(3) holds with
j=2
or
3
.

Case (3).
We have
p\nmid D
by Lemma
9.7
. Comparing (
9.16
) and Theorem
1.6
(1), we need to prove (
9.17
). Let
0\leq z_{1}\leq p-1
be the unique solution of (
9.13
) (the uniqueness follows from
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=0
). Then
z_{1}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
by the assumptions of Case (3), and hence

(9.19)

S_{2}=\left\{z\in\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}\colon 4a_{11}a_{22}-(a_{12}-dz)^{2}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}\right\}\cap\{0,z_{1}\}.

It follows that

(9.20)

\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_0}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}.

By (
9.13
),
a_{13}a_{23}\equiv 2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz_{1})\bmod{p}
, and hence

(9.21)

-4a_{33}^{2}D_{z_{1}}\equiv 4a_{33}^{2}((a_{12}-dz_{1})^{2}-4a_{11}a_{22})\equiv a_{13}^{2}a_{23}^{2}-16a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^{2}\bmod{p}.

Since
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=0
, we assume, without loss of generality,
A_{11}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, that is,
a_{23}^{2}\equiv 4a_{22}a_{33}\bmod{p}
. Then

(9.22)

a_{13}^{2}a_{23}^{2}-16a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^{2}\equiv a_{13}^{2}\cdot 4a_{22}a_{33}-16a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^{2}\equiv-a_{23}^{2}A_{22}\bmod{p}.

Combining (
9.21
) and (
9.22
) we find that

(9.23)

\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-4a_{33}^2D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{23}^2}{p}_{K}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}.

The last equality follows from the fact
a_{23}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, since
p\mid A_{11}
but
p\nmid 4a_{22}a_{33}
. Inserting (
9.23
) into (
9.20
), and noting that
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}=0
, we arrive at (
9.17
), as desired.

Case (4).
We have
p\mid D
by Lemma
9.7
. Since
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
, (
9.13
) has two different solutions
z\equiv z_{0},z_{1}\bmod{p}
. (Assume that
0\leq z_{0}<z_{1}\leq p-1
.) Since
A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, we have
z_{0}=0
. Thus,
S_{2}
is still given by (
9.19
), and hence (
9.20
) holds as well in this case. Since
z=z_{1}
satisfies (
9.13
), and since
A_{11}A_{22}\equiv A_{12}^{2}\bmod{p}
, we have
a_{13}a_{23}-2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz_{1})\equiv\pm A_{12}\bmod{p}
. The plus sign cannot occur, since
z_{1}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Therefore,
a_{33}dz_{1}\equiv-A_{12}\bmod{p}
, and hence

(9.24)

\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-a_{33}^2D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^2-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^2}{p}_{K}.

We have

\displaystyle 4A_{33}((a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^{2}-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^{2})

\displaystyle=4A_{33}(-A_{33}a_{33}^{2}+a_{13}a_{23}A_{12})

\displaystyle=-4(a_{33}A_{33})^{2}+4a_{13}a_{23}(A_{13}A_{23}-Da_{12})

(9.25)

\displaystyle\equiv-(2a_{33}A_{33})^{2}+4(a_{13}A_{13})(a_{23}A_{23})\bmod{p},

where we have used the second equation of (
9.2
) and the fact
p\mid D
in this case. By (
9.7
) and
p\mid D
we find that
(2a_{33}A_{33})^{2}\equiv(a_{13}A_{13}+a_{23}A_{23})^{2}
, from which and (
9.25
) we deduce

4A_{33}((a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^{2}-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^{2})\equiv-(a_{13}A_{13}-a_{23}A_{23})^{2}\bmod{p}.

It follows that

(9.26)

\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^2-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^2}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}A_{13}-a_{23}A_{23})^2}{p}_{K}.

Taking the difference between (
9.5
) and (
9.6
), and noting that
a_{11}A_{11}\not\equiv a_{22}A_{22}\bmod{p}
(one of the assumptions of Case (4)), we obtain
a_{13}A_{13}-a_{23}A_{23}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. This, together with (
9.26
), implies
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^2-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^2}{p}_{K}\neq 0
. Combining this, (
9.24
), and (
9.20
) (which we have shown is true as well in this case), we find that

(9.27)

\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}=2\cdot\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}.

By the assumption
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
, we know that
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}
. Since
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}\neq 0
and
p\mid D
, we conclude from the first equation of (
9.2
) that
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{22}A_{33}}{p}_{K}=1
, so
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{jj}}{p}_{K}
are the same for
j=1,2,3
. Thus, we are in the case of Theorem
1.6
(3). The desired conclusion follows from inserting (
9.27
) into (
9.16
), and taking into account the fact
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{jj}}{p}_{K}
are the same.

Case (5).
We have
p\nmid D
by Lemma
9.7
. As in Case (4), (
9.13
) has two different solutions
z\equiv z_{0},z_{1}\bmod{p}
. (Again we assume that
0\leq z_{0}<z_{1}\leq p-1
.) The difference is that in the present case we have
z_{0},z_{1}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
since
A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Therefore

(9.28)

S_{2}=\left\{z\in\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}\colon 4a_{11}a_{22}-(a_{12}-dz)^{2}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}\right\}\cap\{0,z_{0},z_{1}\},

and hence

(9.29)

\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_0}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}.

We shall prove

(9.30)

\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_0}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}\neq 0.

Set

(9.31)

Z_{j}=4a_{33}^{2}(a_{12}-dz_{j})^{2}-16a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^{2}\equiv-4a_{33}^{2}D_{z_{j}}\bmod{p},\quad j=0,1.

Since
z\equiv z_{0},z_{1}\bmod{p}
are solutions of (
9.13
), we have

(9.32)

Z_{j}\equiv-2a_{13}a_{23}(a_{13}a_{23}-2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz_{j}))+A_{11}A_{22}+a_{13}^{2}a_{23}^{2}-16a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^{2},\quad j=0,1.

Multiplying
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
, and using the fact
a_{13}a_{23}-2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz_{0})\equiv-(a_{13}a_{23}-2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz_{1}))\bmod{p}
, we have

(9.33)

Z_{0}Z_{1}\equiv 16a_{33}^{2}(a_{13}^{2}a_{22}-a_{23}^{2}a_{11})^{2}\bmod{p}.

By the assumption
a_{11}A_{11}\not\equiv a_{22}A_{22}\bmod{p}
, we have
p\nmid a_{13}^{2}a_{22}-a_{23}^{2}a_{11}
. It follows that
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{Z_0}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{Z_1}{p}_{K}\neq 0
, and hence
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_0}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}\neq 0
. The assumption
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
implies
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}\neq 0
. Therefore, to obtain (
9.30
), it remains to prove
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{Z_1}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}
. We have

\displaystyle(a_{23}A_{22}+a_{13}(a_{13}a_{23}-2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz_{1})))^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle a_{23}^{2}A_{22}^{2}+a_{13}^{2}A_{11}A_{22}+2a_{13}a_{23}A_{22}(a_{13}a_{23}-2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz_{1}))

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-A_{22}Z_{1}\bmod{p},

where the first congruence follows from the fact
z\equiv z_{1}\bmod{p}
is a solution of (
9.13
), and the second congruence follows from (
9.32
). It follows that
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{Z_1}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}
. Therefore, (
9.30
) holds. Since
p\nmid D
, we are in the case of Theorem
1.6
(1). The conclusion follows by combining (
9.16
), (
9.29
), and (
9.30
).

In the remaining cases (Cases (6)–(15)), we should use (
9.15
) instead of (
9.16
).

Case (6).
We have
p\mid D
by Lemma
9.7
. Repeating
12
12

12

This is reasonable since the beginning part of the deduction of Case (4) only uses the assumptions
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
and
A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, which are also assumptions of Case (6).
the beginning part of the deduction of Case (4), we have (
9.20
) and (
9.24
)–(
9.26
). In particular,

(9.34)

\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^2-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^2}{p}_{K}.

Since
A_{23}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
(one of the assumptions) and
D\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, we have
A_{33}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
by the first equation of (
9.2
). Since
a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22}\bmod{p}
(one of the assumptions), the right-hand side of (

) is equal to
0
, and hence
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^2-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^2}{p}_{K}=0
. Therefore, (

) becomes

(9.35)

\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}.

Now we consider the sum
\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right)
in (
9.15
). We must have
S_{1}\neq\emptyset
, for
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
and
a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22}\bmod{p}
by the assumptions. Let
z\in S_{1}
. We have just proved
A_{33}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, so
z\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Setting
G=G_{z}
,
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{z}
in Lemma
9.4
, we find that
g_{12}v_{1}-2g_{11}v_{2}\equiv-da_{13}z^{2}+A_{23}z\bmod{p}
. If

, then

by Lemma
9.4
(1). Otherwise, if

, then

. Since
a_{13}\not\equiv 0,\,A_{23}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
, we have
r_{z}-p=p\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}
by Lemma
9.4
(2). It follows that

(9.36)

\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right)=p\cdot\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}.

Inserting (
9.35
) and (
9.36
) into (
9.15
), we obtain the conclusion of Theorem
1.6
(3) with
j=1
. However, the conclusion with
j=2,3
holds as well, since in Case (6)
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{jj}}{p}_{K}
are the same for all

. (For instance,
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}
follows from the first equation of (
9.2
).)

Case (7).
This never occurs by Lemma
9.7
.

Case (8).
We have
p\mid D
by Lemma
9.7
. The only difference between this case and Case (6) is the assumption on
A_{23}
. Consequently, some part of the deduction of Case (6) is valid here: (
9.34
) holds, and
S_{1}\neq\emptyset
. We have
A_{33}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
by the first equation of (
9.2
). Furthermore, we have
A_{13}\equiv 0
and
A_{12}\not\equiv 0
mod
p
, by the first equations of (
9.3
) and (
9.4
). Moreover, it follows from (
9.6
) that
a_{12}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Now we have

\displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^2-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^2}{p}_{K}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-a_{33}^2A_{33}+a_{13}a_{23}A_{12}}{p}_{K}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{13}a_{23}A_{12}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{12}a_{13}a_{23}\cdot a_{12}A_{12}}{p}_{K}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{12}a_{13}a_{23}\cdot(-2a_{11}A_{11})}{p}_{K}\quad\text{ (by \eqref{eq:Dexpansion1})}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}(a_{12}a_{13})^2}{p}_{K}.

Since
a_{13}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
(the first time we use this assumption in this case!), we have

\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^2-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^2}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}(a_{12}a_{13})^2}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}.

Inserting this into (
9.34
), we obtain

(9.37)

\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}.

For the sum
\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right)
, we use a reasoning similar to Case (6), whose details are omitted here. We obtain that

(9.38)

\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right)=0.

Inserting (
9.37
) and (
9.38
) into (
9.15
), and noting that
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}=0
, we obtain the conclusion of Theorem

(3) with
j=2
. However, the conclusion with
j=1
also holds since
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
.

Case (9).
We have
p\mid D
by Lemma
9.7
. Most of the assumptions are the same to Case (8), so we can repeat the deduction there until we need the assumption on
a_{13}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^2-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^2}{p}_{K}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{(a_{13}a_{23}-a_{12}a_{33})^2-4a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^2}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{a_{13}a_{23}A_{12}}{p}_{K}

(9.39)

\displaystyle=0.

In the last equality, we have used the assumption
a_{13}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Now we consider the sum
\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right)
in (
9.15
). We must have
\lvert S_{1}\rvert=2
, for
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
and
a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22}\bmod{p}
by the assumptions. Since
A_{33}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
(one of the assumptions), we have
0\in S_{1}
. Assume that
S_{1}=\{0,z_{0}\}
, where
z_{0}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Then
a_{12}-dz_{0}\equiv-(a_{12}-d\cdot 0)\bmod{p}
, and hence
dz_{0}\equiv 2a_{12}\bmod{p}
. Setting
G=G_{z_{0}}
,
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{z_{0}}
in Lemma
9.4
, we find that

g_{12}v_{1}-2g_{11}v_{2}\equiv-da_{13}z_{0}^{2}+A_{23}z_{0}\equiv 0\bmod{p},

where the last congruence follows from the assumptions
a_{13}\equiv A_{23}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Therefore, we deduce from Lemma
9.4
(2) that
r_{z_{0}}-p=p\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}
. Similarly, we have
r_{0}-p=p\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{0}{p}_{K}=0
. Thus,

(9.40)

\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right)=(r_{0}-p)+(r_{z_{0}}-p)=p\cdot\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}.

Inserting (
9.39
) and (
9.40
) into (
9.15
), and noting that
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
and
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{33}}{p}_{K}=0
, we obtain the conclusion of Theorem
1.6
(3).

Case (10).
This never occurs by Lemma
9.7
.

Case (11).
We have
p\nmid D
by Lemma
9.7
. The congruence (
9.13
) has two different solutions
z\equiv z_{0},z_{1}\bmod{p}
and we assume that
0\leq z_{0}<z_{1}\leq p-1
. Since
A_{11}A_{22}-A_{12}^{2}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
we have
z_{0},z_{1}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Therefore, (
9.28
), and hence (
9.29
) hold. We define
Z_{0}
,
Z_{1}
by (
9.31
). Then (
9.32
) and (
9.33
) hold in the present case as well. Since
a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22}\bmod{p}
(one of the assumptions), we have
a_{13}^{2}a_{22}-a_{23}^{2}a_{11}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. This, together with (
9.33
), implies that
Z_{0}Z_{1}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
Z_{1}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Set
t=a_{13}a_{23}-2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz_{0})
, then
t^{2}\equiv A_{11}A_{22}\bmod{p}
and
a_{13}a_{23}-2a_{33}(a_{12}-dz_{1})\equiv-t\bmod{p}
. By (
9.32
), we have
Z_{1}-Z_{0}\equiv 4a_{13}a_{23}t\bmod{p}
. However, (congruences below are modulo
p
)

\displaystyle 2a_{13}a_{23}t

\displaystyle\equiv-A_{11}A_{22}-a_{13}^{2}a_{23}^{2}+16a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}^{2}\quad(\text{since }Z_{1}\equiv 0\text{ and \eqref{eq:proofThm1.6Temp14}})

\displaystyle=4a_{33}(a_{11}A_{11}+a_{22}A_{22})-2A_{11}A_{22}

\displaystyle\equiv 8a_{33}a_{11}A_{11}-2A_{11}A_{22}\quad(\text{since }a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22})

\displaystyle=2a_{13}^{2}A_{11}.

It follows that

\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{Z_0}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-(Z_1-Z_0)}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-4a_{13}^2A_{11}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K},

where we have used (the first time in this case) the assumption
a_{13}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_0}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{Z_0}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{Z_1}{p}_{K}

(9.41)

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}.

For
\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right)
, (
9.36
) holds as well for the present case. This can be proved using an argument similar to that of Case (6), and we omit it. (Note that
A_{33}
can be either zero or nonzero modulo
p
in this case.) Inserting (
9.41
) and (
9.36
) into (
9.15
), we obtain the conclusion of Theorem
1.6
(1) in the present case.

Cases (12)–(14).
These never occur by Lemma
9.7
.

Case (15).
We have
p\nmid D
by Lemma
9.7
. The first three assumptions are the same to Case (11), so we can repeat the deduction there until we need the assumption on
a_{13}
. In this way we obtain
Z_{0}Z_{1}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
and

Z_{1}-Z_{0}\equiv 4a_{13}a_{23}t\equiv 0\bmod{p}.\quad(\text{since }a_{13}\equiv 0\bmod{p})

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in S_{2}}\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_z}{p}_{K}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_0}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-D_{z_1}}{p}_{K}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{Z_0}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{Z_1}{p}_{K}

(9.42)

\displaystyle=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{p}_{K}.

Now we consider the sum
\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right)
in (
9.15
). We must have
\lvert S_{1}\rvert=2
, for
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{A_{11}A_{22}}{p}_{K}=1
and
a_{11}A_{11}\equiv a_{22}A_{22}\bmod{p}
by the assumptions. As
A_{33}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
(one of the assumptions), we have
0\not\in S_{1}
. Assume that
S_{1}=\{z_{2},z_{3}\}
, where
z_{2},z_{3}\not\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Setting
G=G_{z_{2}}
,
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{z_{2}}
in Lemma
9.4
, we find that

g_{12}v_{1}-2g_{11}v_{2}\equiv-da_{13}z_{2}^{2}+A_{23}z_{2}\equiv 0\bmod{p},

where the last congruence follows from the assumptions
a_{13}\equiv A_{23}\equiv 0\bmod{p}
. Therefore, we deduce from Lemma
9.4
(2) that
r_{z_{2}}-p=p\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}z_2^2}{p}_{K}=p\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}
. Similarly, we have
r_{z_{3}}-p=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}
. However,
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}=\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}
by the first assumption, so

\sum_{z\in S_{1}}\left(r_{z}-p\right)=(r_{z_{2}}-p)+(r_{z_{3}}-p)=p\cdot\left(\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{p}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{p}_{K}\right).

Inserting this and (
9.42
) into (
9.15
), we obtain the conclusion of Theorem
1.6
(1), which concludes the whole proof.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

Of course, one can produce an independent proof using the idea of
[
5
, §V.1]
. In our opinion, such a proof might not be simpler than ours, since it as well need to compute four
r_{G,\mathbf{v}}(m)
, where
\det(G)
may be or may not be congruence to
0
modulo
p
. Thus, one still need to split the proof into many cases.

10.
Miscellaneous observations and open questions

Formulas for
\mathfrak{G}_{G}(a/c;\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x})
for arbitrary
c
.
Theorem
1.1
gives formulas in the case
\gcd(N,c)=1
or
N
. As we have mentioned, by combining Lemma
3.7
and
[
46
, Remark 6.8]
, one can deduce a formula for all positive integers
c
. However, this formula involves quantities like
\xi(a,c)
(cf.
[
46
, Definition 6.1]
), which requires firstly decomposing
G^{-1}\mathbb{Z}^{n}/\mathbb{Z}^{n}
into Jordan components. It is natural further work to derive a formula for all
c
that does not involve any local data. Such formulas exist for
n=1
(Gauss) and
n=2
(Skoruppa and Zagier
[
42
, Theorem 3]
).

On the positive definiteness restriction.
Theorem
1.1
requires
G
to be positive definite. We have removed this restriction in Theorem
4.6
for
\gcd(N,c)=1
. It seems that we can merely assume
G
is nonsingular in all results (except Theorem
7.4
). However, in some places in these generalized formulas, the rank
n
should be replaced by the signature; see e.g. Remark
3.22
. (Although in Theorem
4.6
, the formula remains unchanged compared to the positive definite case.) It is our further work to remove this restriction.

Non-integral-parametric sums.
Theorem
1.1
is concerned with
\mathfrak{G}_{G}(a/c;\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x})
of integral-parametric (see Definition
3.1
). We do not know any explicit formula if it is non-integral-parametric. Here we pose an open question: derive an explicit formula for
\mathfrak{G}_{G}(a/c;\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x})
where
G\cdot\mathbf{w}\not\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
or
a\cdot\mathbf{x}\not\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

Gauss sums in finite fields.
As we have shown in (
1.3
), Gauss sums in finite fields can be reduced to
\mathfrak{G}_{G}(a/c;\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x})
of integral-parametric. It is an interesting problem to derive, using Theorem
1.1
, explicit formulas for the following sum

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\beta_{1}x^{2}+\beta_{2}x)}{p}\right).\qquad(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q})

This has potential applications to the subject of, e.g., determining the zeta functions of varieties of characteristic
p
.

Hecke Gauss sums.
We have discussed this topic in §
6.4
and have shown that Hecke Gauss sums can always be reduced to
\mathfrak{G}_{G}(a/c;\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x})
of integral-parametric (see Proposition
6.3
).Then we discussed the cases of quadratic fields and cyclotomic fields in more details. A natural direction of further work is to deduce more explicit formulas for Hecke Gauss sums in other number fields.

Applications of the duality theorem.
Theorem
6.2
is an application of Theorem
1.3
. A problem: find an explicit formula for

\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}/c\mathbb{Z}^{n},\,\mathbf{v}\perp\mathbf{h_{1}},\mathbf{h_{2}}}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{a}{c}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{w})^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot G\cdot(\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{w})\right)\right).

This could be done as well by applying Theorem
1.3
.

Affine quadratic hypersurfaces.
A problem: generalize Theorem
1.5
to the modulus
p^{r}
. This might be done by applying Theorem
8.1
with
c
set to
p^{r}
. Note that Li and Ouyang
[
25
]
have achieved this, among other beautiful results, for diagonal
G
. Another related problem is to generalize Theorem
1.5
to the corresponding Diophantine equation over
\mathbb{F}_{p^{r}}
.

Generalized Markoff equations over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
Theorem
1.6
is concerned with the equation (
1.12
) over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. It might be more involved to prove a formula for the number of solutions of (
1.12
) over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, where
q=p^{r}
, a prime power. (But for a fixed equation with
a_{ij}
and
d
known, this can be done directly by the method of Baragar
[
5
, §V.1]
.) Motivated by numerical experiments, we pose the following conjecture.

Conjecture 10.1
.

Suppose that
p
is a prime with
p\nmid a_{11}a_{22}a_{33}d
and
r
is a positive integer. Set
q=p^{r}
.

(1) If
p\nmid D
, then
p>2
, and the number of solutions of (
1.12
), where
x,y,z\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
, equals

q^{2}+\left(\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{q}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{q}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{q}_{K}\right)\cdot q+1.

(2) If
2<p\mid D
and at least two of
A_{11},A_{22},A_{33}
are divisible by
p
, then the solution number over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is

q^{2}+1.

(3) If
2<p\mid D
and at most one of
A_{11},A_{22},A_{33}
is divisible by
p
, then the solution number over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is

q^{2}+\left(\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{11}}{q}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{22}}{q}_{K}+\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{33}}{q}_{K}-\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{-A_{jj}}{q}_{K}\right)\cdot q+1,

where
1\leq j\leq 3
is any index such that
p\nmid A_{jj}
.

(4) If
2=p\mid D
, then the solution number over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is

\begin{cases}q^{2}+1&\text{ if }a_{12}\equiv a_{13}\equiv a_{23}\equiv 0\bmod{2}\\
q^{2}+(-1)^{r}2q+1&\text{ if }a_{12}\equiv a_{13}\equiv a_{23}\equiv 1\bmod{2}\\
q^{2}+(-1)^{r}q+1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}
